#6941. Ezra Frech and Connor Wood Are the Final Boys

The last two men standing on DWTS talk beating expectations, blocking out the noise, and what happens to their bromance after the ballroom.

One of the best things to come out of Dancing With the Stars season 35? After Guillermo Rodriguez’s assisted knee walks and the chaotic Cat in the Hat dance routine following Tyler Cameron’s baby gender reveal, it’s got to be Ezra Frech and Connor Wood’s unexpected bromance.

Despite premiering over 21 years ago, the show is currently experiencing a major modern resurgence, and social media engagement is to thank. The show has tapped into younger audiences both with its casting choices and through daily behind-the-scenes clips, to the point where even A-listers like Jennifer Lawrence can recognize Connor drinking a smoothie on the street. (By the way, if you’re wondering whether J. Law has reached out to him since he paid her no mind, the answer is sadly no. “I beat that horse to death,” Connor says. “I’m sure she’s like, ‘Can this guy let it go?’”)

And it’s also thanks to social media that fans have gotten a front-row seat to Connor and Ezra’s off-camera bond. What began with a TikTok poking fun at the fact that Connor’s nickname is Fibula and Ezra was born without his left fibula (which was all Ezra’s idea, according to Connor) has blossomed into an iconic friendship. Now, the besties must face off as the two remaining male stars heading into the finale—but they’re not going to let a little competition come between them.

“Ezra is definitely more competitive,” Connor, who’s paired with Rylee Arnold, tells Cosmopolitan. “He’s got a fighting spirit. When we get to eliminations, I’m thinking about my post-show quesadilla. I mean, obviously I want to stay in the competition. But I’ve considered every week that I’ve made it a win. The only person I’m competitive with is myself because I want to get this right.”

“This is a competition show, but you’re kind of rooting for everybody else and want everyone else to do well,” Ezra, who’s partnered with Daniella Karagach, adds. “Plus, there’s so much that’s out of your control. I want Connor to succeed. He wants me to succeed. You know, it’s real love.”

The first time the two met was at the promo photo shoot ahead of the season premiere, where the connection was so instantaneous that Connor thought they must already know each other. As Connor explains, “We’re hugging, we’re chatting, and then I’m walking away like, ‘Oh my gosh, where did I meet him before?’ Turns out, it was our first time meeting. We just connected straight off the bat.”

“Sometimes you’ll meet somebody and just click and know like, ‘Oh, that’s somebody I’m gonna be friends with,’” says Ezra of that fateful first meeting. “I’m sure Connor and I would have definitely hit it off and become really close in any other context—but I think Dancing With the Stars accelerated that timeline because we’re both way out of our comfort zone. For us to be in this together, it’s bonded us in a very unique way.”

They may be a decade apart in age (Connor is 31 as of September, while Ezra was born a month before DWTS first premiered in 2005) and come from two entirely different worlds (Connor is a podcaster and comedian, while Ezra is a two-time Paralympian with two gold medals to prove it), but their friendship? From post-rehearsal dance parties to spray tan dates to trailer talks, it knows no bounds.

Cosmopolitan caught up with Ezra and Connor ahead of the DWTS season 35 finale to chat about beating the odds, blocking out the noise, and what happens after they leave the ballroom.

Connor: I thought there was more to the sentence. I thought I would hear, “Connor and Rylee, come down here and dance for us again.” I literally walked backstage after they called mine and Rylee’s name, and I took off my jacket and just sat on the ground. I was in shock.

Ezra: They do say names are called in no particular order, but when I was standing there with Harry [Shum Jr.], I was just like, “Wow, this is crazy.” No one will tell you that they’re standing up there feeling confident, so when they leave you up there longer, it definitely makes you sweat.

Connor: I’m an extrovert, but it’s very selective. I don’t do emotions. Being on the show, I’ve put out a more vulnerable me. I’ve been sharing things on national TV that I don’t even share with my friends, and it’s been wildly uncomfortable in the best way. You know, I’ve always found work for myself that’s come really easy to me—like, comedy has been natural for me. This is nothing like that. This is the hardest I’ve ever worked in my life, and my brain chemistry has been changed forever because of this.

Ezra: I didn’t come in with any dance experience, and quite frankly, I thought I had natural limitations on what I was capable of on the dance floor. What Dani has brought out of me is almost rewiring the limitations I placed on myself and changing my own expectations of what I can do on a prosthetic—which is crazy, because I’ve lived 21 years and felt like I had a pretty good understanding of what I was capable of on my own prosthetic leg. Then I come on Dancing With the Stars and meet Dani and that completely shifts.

Connor: [laughs] You think I’m watching myself? Oh, no. I don’t watch. And the choreo has been hard, but it’s gotten easier each week for me to learn dances. Like, learning the week 3 samba, I’m like, “Rylee, pack your bags. It’s been great getting to know you.” But now I know that I’ll get it.

Ezra: Yeah, I don’t really watch. I mean, I end up seeing clips, and earlier in the season, I would rewatch the dances. But everything’s happening so fast; it’s hard to, like, really sit and marinate on them when you gotta refocus for the next week. The cool thing is these dances will live forever. They’re out there in the world, and I will be able to watch and appreciate those moments and have pride in them for the rest of my life.

Connor: Michael Scott said it best: “Do I need to be liked? Absolutely not. I like to be liked. I enjoy being liked. I have to be liked.” I relate to that so much, because I’ll want to reach out to someone and be like, “Do you really hate me? Like, walk me through what I did during my jive that has made you just despise me?” I don’t think these people actually have that much hatred in their heart. I think they’re really invested fans, and it’s part of the game. Would I slide into someone’s DMs and say, “Watch your back”? No. But I know it’s what I signed up for.

Ezra: People online—as far as whether they think you’re great or mediocre or suck or whatever the opinions are on social media—shouldn’t influence your day-to-day. Thankfully, for Dani and I, there haven’t been too many negative comments, but, of course, with every 30 positive comments, you’re going to get a negative comment. It’s all about not letting that little negative comment overshadow the beautiful stuff that people are saying online as well.

Connor: This is a once-in-a-lifetime chance, but it’s also objectively fun. It’s a show about dancing. I’m not going to make a joke of it, but there’s humor involved. It’s so important to take a step back and laugh sometimes. This is fun. It should be fun.

Connor: You do it anxious. You know, there are people like Ezra with serious uphill battles, and he’s crushing it. I’m just here with anxiety and ADHD and small amounts of OCD and dancing on TV. It’s just something that I’ve always dealt with. I go into standup anxious, and honestly, I think it helps me operate. It pushes me to make sure I get in all of my rehearsal hours so that there’s nothing to be anxious about. As my dad told me, “If you weren’t anxious, you would be anxious.”

Ezra: I think the way we phrase things in our head controls the way we feel about them. Yes, it can be a burden to feel like you’re representing a community, but it’s a beautiful burden, and so I don’t see it as a negative thing. I get to play a small role in representing my community on such a special show like Dancing With the Stars—I think it’s actually, like, the pride and joy and honor of my life.

Ezra: No, it’s not in my DNA to chill. I mean, after 2028, I’ll probably take some downtime for sure—but then, you know, you’ve got the next Games in 2032, you’ve got world championships the next year, so there’s never really an end. I’ll probably compete on the track for another 15 years or so. And hopefully after the show ends, I’ll take a nice vacation or just rot for a little bit.

Connor: Rylee and Ezra are the same age, and you know, I do feel my age a lot of the time when I talk to them. Like, Rylee only knew who Nicole Kidman was because of the AMC pre-roll ads, and Ezra hadn’t seen High School Musical when we were doing the Wildcats dance. But both of them are so wise beyond their years. As soon as Ezra and I became friends, I started seeing old clips of him at, like, 9 years old on Ellen, and he was probably more eloquent and emotionally mature than I am now. People keep wanting him to be the Bachelor, but I think he needs to run for office. He’s just got this natural charisma.

Ezra: I didn’t listen to his podcast, but I’d seen clips of him online, and I knew of him. There’s a really funny video of a Team USA thing that I did two or three years ago where I’m lip-syncing to Connor’s audio. It’s like, “I have purse; I have shoe.” And now people are like, “Wow, the invisible string theory brought them together.” It’s funny; he’s the exact same online versus in person. He’s the same dude. He’s able to take things in, he’s able to feel them, and then he’s able to articulate them in a way that resonates with people. That’s what makes him so good as a comedian.

Connor: There’s this guy in a suit that’s jumping straight up in the air and it looks like an exclamation point. I’d say that’s Ezra, since he’s so elevated in the way he carries himself. And, of course, he’s got hops.

Ezra: I would describe Connor with the emoji that’s smiling and sweating, because he’s always smiling and he’s always kind of sweating, and it’s sort of perfect. Like, he’ll make jokes and then look around like, “Ooh, did that land?”

Connor: Val [Chmerkovskiy] and Jenna [Johnson] and I are gonna work on hip-hop dance at their studio. Val and I are also doing a standup show together at some point. I just adore Brandon [Armstrong] and Alan [Bersten], too. Us three together? Trouble. Britt [Stewart] and I connected on some zen woo-woo stuff, so we’re gonna hang out. And Jenna Dewan and her partner Steve [Kazee], we’re gonna hang out. Julianne [Hough] and I are gonna hang out in New York. I mean, I talk to everybody. I hope I see everybody again.

Ezra: Amber [Glenn] is someone who is a real close part of my life and will be for a long time. I feel like her and I are on the same team together in this with Pasha [Pashkov] and Dani. Guillermo is someone I’m going to be close to forever. Like me, him, and Connor—we’re in the group chat texting all the time. And I love Tyler Cameron, and Harry is the sweetest, and Jenna—like, it’s hard to pick and choose. I could see myself still talking to pretty much everybody on the cast.

Ezra: The internet is crazy. Right now, I’m definitely single but not necessarily looking for a relationship. Because, again, once the dance season ends, I’ll be committing myself entirely to track again. I’m enjoying being single, and it’ll probably stay that way for a while.

Connor: This has been like a study abroad trip. Like, I am fully immersed in a culture that is so far outside of my comfort zone and so far away from what I know. Learning something new at my age is so rare, let alone at this level on TV with a world-class athlete teaching me. And if it does happen, it probably happens to billionaires’ kids who are, like, paying Serena Williams to do private lessons for two years. I think I’m gonna wake up on Wednesday feeling like I walked out of a loud party and shut the door with my ears still ringing. Like, I’m alone. Party is over. It’s time to go home.

Ezra: I’ll miss getting to see everyone at the rehearsal studio. I’ll miss getting lunch with Dani after practice. I’ll miss getting the opportunity to commit myself to something like this so entirely. And I’m gonna miss the show days. You become so close to everybody, and you spend so much time around these people, and then all of a sudden, it’s going to end. But the good thing is that these people are still going to be in my life. I’m still going to hang out with Dani, and I’m still going to see Connor whenever I can, and we’ll get lunch with Guillermo. These are people I’ll be with forever.

Styled by Kaia Carioli. Sittings editor: Marissa Diaz. Grooming by Jaime Maloney for Bumble and Bumble.

#6936. Jenna Johnson Called Out “Online Bullying” and “Hate” from ‘Dancing With the Stars’ Fans

“Hate has become so relevant and intertwined with the show.”

It’s been almost a week since Jenna Johnson and Harry Shum Jr.’s shocking elimination from Dancing With the Stars, and now that she’s had some time to process, Jenna says she’s hoping fans of the show can get back to spreading joy instead of online bullying.

“I feel like Dancing with the Stars used to be the show that everyone tuned into to turn off the noise and to just experience two hours of pure joy, and entertainment, and excitement,” she told People. “So it’s sad that this online bullying and hate has become so relevant and intertwined with the show.”

Jenna certainly isn’t the only person on Dancing With the Stars season 35 to speak out about the increasingly negative vibes on social media.

Harry previously told E! News, “I hope they address that at a certain point. Because I think there’s a good amount of bullying that doesn’t feel really good, and I think the rest of the cast feels it.”

Meanwhile, Jenna Dewan told Extra when she was eliminated, “I know it’s hard to know how this all plays into it. I know this was a particularly tough week for a lot of the online noise and all stuff that comes with the show. I don’t know, I’ll never know how, or if, any of that plays into this. But I know that we went out there and had the best time of our life.”

Plenty of shows have had to issue anti-bullying PSAs to their audience ahead of the show (including Love Island and The Summer I Turned Pretty), so it’s possible Dancing With the Stars will follow suit next season.

#6935. AI Native Right Hand of the CEO - EdTech (French fluent 🇫🇷) at Paradox

You dream of a new challenge, of being able to make a company scale and feel that you have a real impact.

If you dream of a life that is more like an adventure than a stable life, then becoming the AI-native right hand of our CEO is for you.

This isn’t another chief of staff or right hand job.

Most companies call themselves AI-first because they gave everyone a ChatGPT licence. We think that’s level 2, and there are 10.

At Paradox our ambition is $10M in revenue with 10 people, each of them running a dozen agents. Here, AI doesn’t assist the work. It runs the loops, in every department. Your job is to make that true for the whole company, side by side with the CEO.

Deep inside you are looking for a new adventure with…

🗽Freedom: working remotely or hybrid, and being trusted to deliver

🔥Ambition: building one of the first truly AI-native companies, with teammates who push AI to its limits every week

😍Impact: changing the trajectory of a whole company, not one line of a roadmap, with a product that transforms people’s lives at scale

Then, you have found the next adventure that will fulfill you.

In a company of fewer than 10 people, the right hand used to be the person who absorbed everything the CEO couldn’t do. Here, the right hand builds the machine that does it.

Hold the full context of Paradox. Our DNA, offers, decisions, procedures, member stories and past experiments, in one shared company brain that every person and every agent reads before acting.

Turn every decision into execution. A meeting, a voice note or a call with the CEO becomes briefs, tickets, follow-ups and deadlines, without anyone retyping it.

Act across every tool. Not a chatbot in a tab: agents read and write in our whole stack, from code to CRM, billing, analytics, ads, email, calendar and documents.

Ship. Produce the analysis, the contract draft, the page, the offer or the process, and put it live.

Measure with real KPIs. Revenue, cash, margin, retention and the transformation of our members, not activity.

Learn and loop on its own. Keep what works, kill what doesn’t, write the lesson back into the brain, and start the next cycle without waiting for a meeting.

People set the ambition, the taste and the standards. The machine runs the loop, and gets better every week.

This is already real. Our company brain holds our decisions and procedures. Our growth hub tracks every hypothesis and experiment with its result. Agents cut ads from long-form content, prepare webinars and audit our tracking after every live. And our product is AI-native too: Athena, our AI coach, works with members every day, by text and by voice.

We believe self-help is broken. Most people use learning platforms the same way: generic theory, a quick high, no real change.

They deserve more than content and more than motivation.

They deserve a system that knows them, one that adapts, challenges and grows with them.

Human Intelligence (coaches, peers and mentors)

Structured Intelligence (protocols, mental models, rituals)

Technological Intelligence (AI personalization, feedback loops, adaptive journeys)

into one seamless ecosystem: Paradox OS, the Operating System for Human Greatness, augmented with AI.

Paradox was founded by David Laroche, a creator followed by over 2 million people and known for his interviews with icons like Tony Parker, Mike Horn, Richard Branson and Gad Elmaleh. We started in French. We’re building for the world, and for something that will outlive any individual, its founder included.

Here’s the bet: A small team running a self-learning machine can out-test, out-learn and out-grow teams a hundred times its size.

Reporting directly to the founder and CEO, you will help him run and grow the company.

Your scope is as wide as a CEO/COO’s: strategy, operations, finance, legal, people, product marketing and launches.

The difference is how you do it. In a classic company, the right hand coordinates people and AI helps. Here it’s the other way round: agents do the work, you design the system, set the bar and make the calls. With fewer than 10 people, there is no department to hand things to. You build it.

❌The old way: a chief of staff takes notes in meetings, chases people by email and builds the weekly report by hand.

🏴‍☠️At Paradox: you build the agents that turn every CEO decision into execution. Meetings, voice notes and calls become briefs, tickets and follow-ups. Every Monday, the CEO gets one brief: what moved, what’s stuck, and what needs his call.

You decide what the CEO must see and what he never needs to see again. His time goes to vision, product and members, not to coordination.

Depending on your zone of genius, these are examples of strategic projects you could own:

Growth: run experiments with agents that generate, ship and measure them

Partnerships & sponsorships: find and close partners aligned with our brand, for our YouTube channel and events

International: build the machine that turns our French content into English, and open new countries

Launches: take a new offer or product from idea to live

People: find and assess the rare people who join a team of fewer than 10

❌The old way: each new initiative needs a team, a roadmap and three months.

🏴‍☠️At Paradox: one person with the right agents launches an offer, a channel or a country without disrupting the core. That’s what our Pirate Squad means now.

❌The old way: an ops manager processes invoices, chases contracts, onboards people and answers the same support questions every week.

🏴‍☠️At Paradox: finance, legal, HR, admin and customer support each get their agents, their context, their KPIs and their loop. You write the skills, plug them into our tools and write the evals that define "good": a reconciliation that matches to the cent, a contract that respects our playbook, an answer a member is happy with.

You give autonomy step by step: first an agent suggests, then it acts once you approve, then it acts alone within a budget and guardrails. An agent moves up a level only when its track record earns it.

Example: in your first month, you build the agent that reconciles payments, refunds and failed charges every night. In month one it only reports. By month three it fixes the simple cases alone, and you only see the exceptions.

❌The old way: someone builds dashboards in a BI tool, exports them to a spreadsheet at the end of the month, and nobody fully trusts the numbers.

🏴‍☠️At Paradox: agents watch revenue, cash, acquisition, retention, churn and competitors continuously, find where our sources disagree, and explain what changed and why.

Strategy: screen markets and competition, build growth models, explore business models, partnerships and acquisitions. Then challenge, formalize and ensure good execution of the strategy.

At Paradox things move fast, and our CEO is always reading business books and meeting great entrepreneurs. You’ll turn the best strategies and tactics into systems and agents the whole company uses.

❌The old way: lessons live in people’s heads and leave with them.

🏴‍☠️At Paradox: every decision, result and lesson goes back into the company brain, and agents read it before acting. The company never learns the same lesson twice.

You measure the machine itself: the share of work done without a human, the time from decision to execution, the agents that moved up an autonomy level this month, and revenue per person. You push those numbers up.

You help the team become AI-native too: you spot the work still done by hand, and you build the agent or show a teammate how to build it.

And always: our members trust us with their lives. No fake claims, no fake scarcity, data handled with care. The machine can scale, but it doesn’t decide what’s right. People do.

How you’ll know you’re AI-native: you spend your time building agents, writing evals and making calls, not doing the work by hand. The company’s output grows every month while the headcount doesn’t.

Top tier Business or Engineering School (HEC, ESSEC, INSEAD, EM Lyon, EDHEC, ESCP, Stanford, …) or other high-level academic training (Les Mines, X, Sciences Po Paris, etc.)

A wide-scope role behind you: founder, chief of staff, founder’s associate, strategy, consulting or operations in a fast-growing startup

You’ve built with AI and agents yourself: Claude Code, agent SDKs, MCP, automations, APIs, evals. Show us something you built, not a certificate.

Driven with strong work ethic: this position requires a strong commitment and perseverance

Entrepreneurship spirit: able to own a project from scratch, with few resources and little guidance, doing a lot yourself

Builder, not coordinator: you’d rather build the agent than chase a person twice

Taste: you know the difference between an output that works and one that is great, and you hold agents to the second

Growth over ego: you are at ease with regular feedback, obsessed with learning and growing, able to put your ego aside

Analytical and system thinker: you quickly identify and formulate problems, and solve complex ones with data

Excels in communication: both written and verbal

French: native or bilingual. English: fluent, you can hold a detailed conversation with English-speaking colleagues. Our chosen language for written communication is English.

We are inspired by a culture of performance and ambition. Everything is fast-paced and there is a lot of change. You won’t always be told what to do or how to do it.

We tell each other things, even if they are uncomfortable. We value growth over ego. At Paradox, expressing disagreement to a manager and knowing how to debate is valued! We encourage critical and strategic thinking.

❌ If you use AI for first drafts but your way of working looks the same as two years ago: wrong role.

❌ If you want to manage a lot of people: we aim for $10M in revenue with 10 people, each managing a dozen agents.

❌ If you need clarity before chaos: wrong place.

If all this makes sense with you, then we’ll love working with you (and you with us, you’ll be pampered!)

Build one of the first truly AI-native companies from inside the CEO’s office, and write the playbook others will copy. You’ll compete with San Francisco companies.

🏅Being surrounded by A-players who strive to perform, whilst being in a good mood

🏋️‍♀️No more bosses, but ongoing conversations to support your growth

⚡️Learning to build with AI faster than almost anywhere else

🦄Playing a crucial role in the development of a company whose content and products are used by millions of people

🤩Waking up in the morning feeling like you are making a difference is something! We receive messages every day from members who quit smoking, reached a milestone in their business, built better relationships and changed their lives.

But that’s not all, we believe that driven people deserve…

😎A company culture based on autonomy & accountability

🗽Flexible working hours (but very hard working)

🌎A remote culture from day one with the freedom to work from wherever you want

🧍‍♀️Standing desks paid by the company if you live far from our offices

🏀350€ per year paid by the company to take care of yourself (for your gym, a dance class or whatever makes you feel great)

📚150€ of books or education: at Paradox, we are learner lovers

🔥Access to Athena, our AI coach, supporting you on anything personal or professional

❌ Any copy-pasted and non-personalized application goes directly to the trash can.

⚠️ To complete the recruitment process, each candidate must organize calls with his or her previous managers. If you are not comfortable with this: do not apply ⚠️

📃 A 6 to 8 minute application survey, with a short video and a link to something you built with AI

💻 A 30-minute video call with Timy, HR, to deep dive into your career path and ambitions

🛠️ A live build session: you solve a real Paradox problem with AI in front of us. We look at how you prompt, push back on the output and iterate, more than at a polished result.

💻 A 1h culture-fit video call, to assess alignment with our values and mindset

💻 A 1h final video call with David, CEO, on the company’s next milestones and how you’d build them

💼 Reference calls with your previous managers

If all this sounds good to you, come and write the next chapter with us 🚀🚀

New remote jobs delivered to your inbox, every week.

Location independence is the future of work. It serves companies with best talent from around the globe. And benefits employees with a greater work life balance and the ability to work in their pyjamas.- Nithur, founder.

#6932. Software Engineer - Interfaces Backend at Supabase

Supabase is the Postgres development platform, built by developers for developers. We provide a complete backend solution including Database, Auth, Storage, Edge Functions, Realtime, and Vector Search. All services are deeply integrated and designed for growth.

We’re looking for a Software Engineer to join Supabase’s Interfaces group and help run the backend services behind our developer tools, starting with postgres-meta: the open-source service that runs SQL against customers’ Postgres databases on behalf of our dashboard, our MCP server, AI agents, and other platform tools. You’ll work closely with the engineers who build those tools, and with our Platform and SRE teams.

AI agents are the fastest-growing source of postgres-meta’s traffic, and it sits on the path between developers and their data, so when it struggles, people can’t reach their databases from the dashboard or their agents. The team’s first priority is making it reliable, observable, and fair between tenants at agent scale, and helping redesign it for that scale without breaking the tools that depend on it. Depending on your experience, you’ll own major parts of that work or lead it. Alongside it, the team builds the backend services that give every branch of a project the configuration and secrets it needs.

We want to be upfront: this role carries real operational load. Our on-call process and rotation are new and need a lot of maturing, and you’ll help build them into something sustainable from your first weeks. You’ll be on call for services on the critical path, and incidents come before planned work. Platform teams run the infrastructure underneath; you’ll own the services themselves, from their code to how they behave in production. It’s ideal for someone who enjoys that kind of ownership, thrives in async, fast-paced environments, and stays calm when the root cause turns out to be in someone else’s system.

Help mature the team’s on-call from your first weeks: grow the rotation to cover every service the team runs, write runbooks, and agree when to declare an incident. Then build it, with our embedded SRE, into a sustainable follow-the-sun rotation as the team grows.

Set SLOs with the teams that call the service, and build the observability to hold them: metrics per tenant and per caller, alerts that catch failures wherever they start, and logs you can search across regions.

Make deploys routine with canary and staged rollouts, so dependency and security upgrades stop being risky.

Redesign how the service works with Postgres at scale: reusing connections, limiting or streaming large results, and isolating and rate-limiting tenants, so no single caller, human or agent, can degrade it for everyone else.

Help decide the service’s long-term architecture with our Platform teams, then evolve it without breaking the callers that depend on it.

Write down and agree the service’s contract with its callers, including the dashboard, MCP and AI, and platform workers: how transactions, multi-statement queries, timeouts, and errors behave.

Work through incidents with the networking, Kubernetes, pooler, and control-plane teams when a problem on the path to customers’ databases starts in their systems.

Build backend services for code-first workflows, starting with delivering each branch’s configuration and secrets to the services that need them.

Make small changes directly in the CLI, dashboard, or docs when a capability needs them, with the owning team reviewing.

Review code and designs, mentor other engineers, and grow into leading the team’s technical direction.

Have 5+ years of experience as a software engineer, most of it building and running backend services in production.

Have run a high-traffic, multi-tenant service in production: been on call for it, worked incidents through to resolution, and followed through on postmortems.

Write strong TypeScript, and work confidently in a large Node.js backend codebase.

Understand Postgres from the client side: connection management and pooling, transactions, statement timeouts and cancellation, and large result sets.

Have built observability for a service you owned (metrics, tracing, logs, and alerts tied to SLOs), and used it to debug problems that span proxies, load balancers, or Kubernetes.

Have kept a service healthy under load with patterns such as rate limiting, timeouts, memory limits, and graceful degradation.

Have shipped changes to a live service safely, with canaries, staged rollouts, feature flags, or fast rollback.

Work calmly and constructively with other teams during incidents, including when the root cause is in a system you don’t own.

Communicate clearly in writing, from design docs to postmortems, thrive in async, globally distributed teams, and make progress when the path isn’t fully defined.

Nice to have, roughly in order of how much they’d help:

Have rebuilt or migrated a live, high-traffic service without breaking its callers, using techniques such as shadow traffic, contract tests, or incremental replacement.

Have led the design of reliability work, such as tenant isolation, load shedding, or a staged-deploy pipeline, or set technical direction that other engineers built on.

Have built or operated a database proxy or connection pooler (such as PgBouncer, Supavisor, or pgcat), or an API gateway such as Envoy.

Have secured a service that runs SQL on its callers’ behalf: handling credentials safely, and limiting how much damage any one caller can do.

Production experience in a second backend language, such as Elixir or Go.

Experience with a secrets manager or key management service, such as HashiCorp Vault or AWS KMS.

Familiarity with Supabase, or with how AI agents use databases through tools like MCP.

We hire globally. We believe you can do your best work from anywhere. There are no Supabase offices, but we provide a WeWork membership or co-working allowance you can use anywhere in the world.

Every team member receives ESOP (equity ownership) in the company. We want everyone to share in the upside of what we’re building together.

Use this budget to set up your ideal work environment—laptop, monitor, headphones, or whatever helps you do your best work.

Supabase covers 100% of health insurance for employees and 80% for dependents, wherever you are. Your wellbeing and your family’s health are important to us.

Once a year, the entire company gathers in a new city for a week of connection, collaboration, and fun. It’s a highlight of our year.

We operate asynchronously and trust you to manage your own time. You know what needs to be done and when.

Every team member receives an annual education allowance to spend on learning—courses, books, conferences, or anything that supports your growth.

Supabase was born-remote and open-source-first. We believe our globally distributed team is our secret weapon in building tools developers love.

#6921. Sharna Burgess Calls Out ‘Dancing With the Stars’ Voting System for Letting Fans “Manipulate the Show”

Dancing With the Stars operates on a pretty simple scoring system that pretty much boils down to one part judges and one part audience. But pro Sharna Burgess thinks fans are using their online votes to manipulate the show and vote off talented dancers that shouldn’t be going home.

Speaking on Instagram Stories after Harry Shum Jr. and Jenna Johnson were eliminated, Sharna said (per E! News), “The online discourse is so wild that [it] was pretty clear who fans were ‘voting off’ and how they have felt about dance experience contestants.”

“[It’s] so disappointing to see our voting system is now being used by viewers to vote people off instead of voting for your favorite,” she added. “I say we got [sic] back to yall only have 10 votes total and you have to figure out how to dish them out. This voting ppl off thing is so insanely negative and not what we are built on and it’s seemingly given fans the chance to manipulate the show. JMO.”

There’s been a lot of discourse about the Dancing With the Stars voting system in light of Jenna Dewan and Harry Shum Jr. both going home despite delivering flawless routines and getting high judges’ scores. Jenna even alluded to the online discourse playing a role in her elimination, telling Extra, “I know it’s hard to know how this all plays into it. I know this was a particularly tough week for a lot of the online noise and all stuff that comes with the show. I don’t know, I’ll never know how, or if, any of that plays into this. But I know that we went out there and had the best time of our life.”

Dancing With the Stars producers haven’t addressed voting concerns or indicated that they’re making any changes, but stay tuned.

#6918. Where Is Karen Read Now? Netflix’s Latest True Crime Doc Revisits the John O’Keefe Case

A new three-part true crime documentary on Netflix unpacks the case.

Between the Christmas ads on TV and the colder temps that have made our morning commutes ever-so-slightly more unpleasant, it’s safe to say that winter has arrived. That calls for more time spent at home, snuggled up on the sofa with a good true crime series to binge. And speaking of, Netflix has us covered there with its latest release, The Trials of Karen Read.

As the name suggests, Netflix’s newest three-part documentary follows the criminal trial of Karen Read. But who is Karen Read? What was she on trial for? And where is Karen Read now? Here’s everything you need to know...

Karen Read, who lived in Mansfield, Massachusetts, is a former financial analyst and professor of finance at Bentley University.

During COVID-19, she dated police officer John O’Keefe. They had previously been involved romantically, but had rekindled things during the pandemic after he reached out to her on social media. “He had reached out to me on Facebook,” she told Nightline. “He said, ‘Hey, blast from the past. How’s things?’”

John O’Keefe was an officer in the Boston police force, a position he’d held for 14 years.

On the night of January 28, 2022, Read drove O’Keefe to the home of Brian Albert, a retired Boston police officer, as the pair planned to catch up. Read stayed in the car outside Albert’s home for 10 minutes as she waited to find out if she should join the gathering, but when she didn’t hear anything, she drove home. Given the time of year, the weather was pretty wintry, with low temperatures, wind, and a lot of snow.

When O’Keefe didn’t return home, Read and some mutual friends went looking for him. In Albert’s driveway, the search party found O’Keefe unconscious and covered in snow. He was pronounced dead after being taken to a nearby hospital. O’Keefe was 46.

A medical examination carried out after O’Keefe’s death found traumatic injuries, including skull fractures, two black eyes, and blood around his nose and mouth, per CNN. His cause of death was listed as “blunt force trauma to the head and hypothermia,” but it couldn’t be concluded whether his death was accidental or a homicide.

Three days after John O’Keefe’s death, Karen Read was arrested on suspicion of manslaughter (which was later upgraded to second-degree murder), motor vehicle homicide, and leaving the scene of a personal injury or crime. She denied all of the allegations against her and pleaded not guilty.

At court, prosecutors put forth that Read was drunk on the night of the alleged crime. They said she was angry at her partner and intentionally hit him with her SUV. Meanwhile, Read’s defense team argued that she was framed for his death and that law enforcement had conspired to cover up who really killed O'Keefe.

As the trial played out in both court and on social media, many theories were thrown about. Some suggested that Read deliberately hit O'Keefe with her car, while others alleged he was killed by someone else at the Albert home. By that account, O’Keefe’s body was dumped in the front garden where it was hidden in the snow.

Theories aside, neither the prosecution nor the defense succeeded in proving what really happened. In July 2024, the jury told the judge they could not reach a unanimous verdict and, as such, a mistrial was declared.

In June 2025, at her second trial, Read was acquitted of second-degree murder and the two lesser charges against her. Instead, Read was convicted of driving while under the influence and sentenced to one year of probation due to having no previous criminal convictions.

Since her second trial, Read has kept a relatively low profile, but she has not stayed out of the legal system altogether. She sued Massachusetts State Police and the town of Canton for negligence and civil conspiracy, arguing that they were biased in their investigation. Read was later awarded $100,000 by the Massachusetts State Police, which agreed to settle her lawsuit.

#6915. I Own 200 Perfumes and Baccarat Rouge Is Still My Favorite of All Time

It's refined and fresh, but also woodsy and sexy...and smells a little bit different on everyone.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

When I first moved to New York, my dream was to become the chicest version of myself. I wanted to Carrie Bradshaw-ify my life, if you will. I vowed to take pilates classes, read literature on the subway, drink extra dirty martinis, and smell like a grown adult. This meant I needed to invest in a ~grown adult~ perfume, and I had my sights (errrr, nose?) set on Maison Francis Kurkdjian's Baccarat Rouge 540.

This was before Baccarat Rouge became an iconic perfume in the eyes of PerfumeTok. I'd go to Nordstrom every week to sneak a few spritzes from the displays. At the time, there was nothing else that smelled like it. There were no other jasmine perfumes laced with sugar and woods. It felt like lightning in a bottle back then.

Fast forward five years, and although I haven’t reached fancy New Yorker-status (yet!), I am a beauty editor with an expert nose. In that time my perfume palette expanded, and so did Baccarat Rouge 540's popularity. What once was known as this $$$$ scent that was worn by royalty and A-listers developed a cult-like following on TikTok for being the sexiest perfume. But does it still hold up to those high standards? After years away, I finally bought my own bottle to see if this ultra-famous scent is still as beautiful as I remember.

Key notes: Saffron, jasmine, amberwood, ambergris, fir resin, cedar, ambroxan, sugar, oakmoss

It’s nearly impossible to put Baccarat Rouge 540 into one box or fragrance category because it has a kaleidoscopic aura to it. It’s airy, spicy, woody, metallic, and warm, but also so much more than these descriptors.

The scent, which launched in 2015, was inspired by the 250th anniversary of luxury crystal brand Baccarat. There's a dazzling quality to the perfume that evokes the shimmery look of Baccarat fine crystal—it's like a champagne toast in the opening, with bright saffron and jasmine that create a twinkling essence. Then, it goes a bit mineral and fresh with amber wood and ambergris. But as it dries down, you get a more woodsy, warm tinge of the fragrance. Amber wood, cedar, and fir resin create depth, but instead of going smoky, sugary notes add a sweetness that brings you right back to that glistening quality.

It is definitely polarizing, though. In reviews, some people say it smells really sweet—faintly of cotton candy or vanilla—and nothing else. Others mention it smells like a fancy hotel lobby that makes them feel like they have their whole life figured out.

When I first saw the price of BR540, my jaw literally dropped. (I was fresh out of college, with an entry-level salary, FYI.) However, the high price tag could make anyone’s eyes bug out of their head. Why on earth is this perfume so expensive? Well, it’s a combination of things, including its potency, performance, rare ingredients, and the hype behind this scent.

First, Maison Francis Kurkdjian uses the highest-quality ingredients—some of which take years to source—to create the immaculate juice. No penny is spared in the final product, from the hefty glass bottle to the strong potion inside. The high-end ingredients are part of the reason why this has such impressive longevity. You’ll still be able to smell this at the end of the day even if you only applied one or two spritzes. And, of course, the celebrity fans, as well as social media hype, have increased demand—and also the price.

Baccarat Rouge 540 really does smell a little bit different on everyone, thanks to the ambroxan note (which is a synthetic compound that has a warm, musky scent that interacts a lot with your skin chemistry, so it smells a bit different on everyone). Once dry, it goes really sweet on my skin. I get all the sugary, jasmine sweetness. It's yummy and gourmand—not really this spicy, fir, woodsy scent that it seems like it'd be from the note breakdown.

It's kinda warm and cozy, but not in that fall-perfume way you'd expect. It reminds me a lot of how my skin would smell after taking a shower. There's a clean, subtle aroma that sits right against my skin. It makes me feel powerful, but not because it's strong or intense. Rather, it simply feels like an enhanced version of my own skin. In my opinion, that's the sexiest way to smell.

One thing's for sure, though: Baccarat Rouge 540 definitely leaves an impression and gets compliments. I spritzed this on at the office—on a day when basically no one was in, by the way—and multiple people from all over our floor came up to my desk to say it smelled good "over here."

It's been years (and a lot of bottles of other, similar scents) since I've played with Baccarat Rouge 540. So, admittedly, I thought I’d feel meh about it at this point. But my obsession has been fully renewed. I can't stop spraying the perfume, and every time I wear it, I smell myself all day long (something that doesn't necessarily happen with many of the other perfumes I own). I've been keeping the bottle by my desk, and I had to move it, because I was spraying it too often and wasting the bottle (lol, even a beauty editor doesn't wanna waste precious BR540).

Here's the thing, though: Obviously, it's expensive as heck. You don't need to spend hundreds of dollars on a perfume. But if you want to invest in a fragrance that you can wear legit all the time, for every occasion, during every single season—I'm a believer in the Baccarat Rouge hype.

Beth Gillette is the beauty editor at Cosmopolitan and has seven years of experience researching, writing, and editing skincare stories that range from reviewing Glossier perfumes to Le Labo Santal 33 dupes. She regularly tests and analyzes perfumes while working with the industry’s top fragrance experts to assess new formulas, brands, and trends.

Mary Honkus is a beauty contributor for Cosmopolitan with over eight years of experience researching, writing, and editing beauty stories. She is an authority in all beauty categories, but has a sweet spot for fragrance with a collection of over 600 scents.

Beth Gillette is the beauty editor at Cosmopolitan, where she covers skincare, makeup, hair, nails, and more across digital and print. She can generally be found in bright eyeshadow furiously typing her latest feature or hemming and hawing about a new product you "have to try." Prior to Cosmopolitan, she wrote and edited beauty content as an Editor at The Everygirl for four years. Follow her on Instagram for makeup selfies and a new hair 'do every few months.

#6910. The Exact Order You Should Apply Your Skincare, According to Dermatologists

Following this routine completely changed our skin for the better.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

The world of skincare can quickly become confusing and overwhelming. Doom-scrolling through SkinTok makes things seem even more complicated. Like, how many steps do you actually need? Which serums are worth using? What products are totally superfluous? If you followed every bit of “foolproof” advice you found on the app, your skin barrier would be compromised and your complexion could look worse than before you went down this rabbit hole.

Well, it’s time to stop mindlessly scrolling and start reading because team Cosmo tapped experts to break down the ideal skincare routine order that’s streamlined and effective for your exact concerns.

Trust us, it's a lot to consider even for beauty editors who spend their entire day testing all of these products, let alone a beginner just trying to get glowy, clear skin or deter fine lines. But step by step, we're gonna make sense of it all, below.

First things first: You do not need a 10-step skincare routine (or even a 5-step routine!). "Less is more when it comes to your skin—I'm all about product reducing," says dermatologist Karan Lal, MD. "In reality, our skin chemistry needs hydration and moisture to maintain a good skin barrier, which you can get from just a few products." And, as virtually all dermatologists agree, those products include the following:

That's it. The three most important skincare products are cleanser, moisturizer, and SPF. It’s not glamorous, but good skin doesn’t need to be (just ask your dermatologist or anyone on the Cosmo beauty team—simple is always better). The more products you layer on, the higher your risk is for irritation, allergic reactions, and breakouts, notes Dr. Lal.

Now, if you're looking to build a slightly more advanced skincare routine, we're totally on board with that, too. Keep reading to learn how to incorporate more skincare steps, and what order they should be applied.

“The rule of thumb when applying skincare is to apply the lightest formula first and the heaviest last since thinner products can’t penetrate thicker products,” says dermatologist Shereene Idriss, MD. Example: Layering a serum (thin) on top of your moisturizer (thick) would render that serum almost ineffective because it can't absorb into your skin when there's an occlusive barrier sitting on top of it.

Please, for the love of the beauty gods, remove your makeup and wash your face before slathering on your skincare products. “For ingredients to absorb properly, you need clean skin that’s totally free of oil and grime,” says dermatologist Mona Gohara, MD, which is where double-cleansing comes in.

Wondering how to double cleanse? The simplest method is to remove your makeup with a cleansing oil or balm (neither is "better" than the other; it's just personal preference). This step will dissolve all the makeup on your face without stripping your skin.

Then, follow that up with a gentle cleanser that works with your skin type. Dry skin should look for creamy, non-foaming face washes with barrier-boosting ingredients like ceramides, hyaluronic acid, and glycerin; while oily skin should try gel-based face washes or gentle foaming cleansers.

Those with acne-prone skin will benefit from a formula with added acne-fighting ingredients, like salicylic acid or benzoyl peroxide. Keep in mind, though: If your skin is sensitive, use soothing formulas loaded with things like colloidal oatmeal, aloe vera, and niacinamide.

It depends, but usually yes. If you have oily or acne-prone skin, then definitely yes—cleansing after you wake up is a good way to get rid of any grime or shine that's collected overnight. However, if you have dry or sensitive skin, you probs don't need to re-wash ahead of the rest of your routine, but if you're a sweaty sleeper, it's always a good idea to freshen your face before the rest of your skincare routine.

Face toners used to be terrible, alcohol-based liquids that burned your face. But today’s (good) toners are either filled with gentle, hydrating ingredients for dry and sensitive skin, or filled with chemical exfoliants to treat acne and bumps. “Toners are not meant to be something that sits around on your skin and feels heavy,” says Dr. Gohara. “They’re just priming your face for ingredients to be better absorbed later on.”

Reminder: Toner (like most of the steps on this list) is optional, but if your toner smells like nail polish remover or alcohol, you need to ditch it—fast—and find an updated version instead. The best toner for acne-prone or oily skin is gonna be filled with ingredients that'll help unclog pores and prevent breakouts, like salicylic acid and/or AHAs. Tap it on your skin every other night (or morning, if you plan to use a retinol at night), then wait 60 seconds before layering another product.

If your skin is dry and/or sensitive, choose a toner with hydrating ingredients to reduce potential irritation (like hyaluronic acid, polyglutamic acid, squalane, ceramides, etc.). You can pat this onto your skin every morning, night, or both.

Toners and essences are pretty interchangeable in their consistencies, so if you’re looking for when to use your essence in your skincare routine, use it as step 2 (instead of toner). If you happen to have both in your routine, swipe on a toner first, then pat in an essence. (Think of it like a thin moisturizer-serum hybrid.)

Exfoliators keep skin smooth and clear by removing built-up dead skin cells and excess oil that can dull your complexion and block product absorption. They should be used after toner, when the skin is clean and balanced, and before serums so your treatments can penetrate more effectively.

There are two types of exfoliators: Chemical and physical. Chemical exfoliants use acids or enzymes (like AHAs, BHAs, or PHAs) to dissolve dead skin cells and are best for concerns like acne, clogged pores, uneven texture, and dark spots. Physical exfoliants use gentle scrubbing particles to manually buff skin smooth and can give instant results, but should be used carefully to avoid irritation.

Incorporate an exfoliator into your routine one to three times per week for ongoing skin concerns, especially if you have sensitive or acne-prone skin. Don’t forget to follow exfoliation with hydrating products and sunscreen during the day, too!

“Serums are essentially shots of extremely concentrated nutrients, hydrators, or antioxidants that really amp up your skin health as soon as you apply them,” says Dr. Gohara. “People often skip out on using them, but they’re honestly the heavy lifters of your skincare routine.” So, while serums are totally optional, they’re often a good first step to leveling up your skincare routine and boosting your skin health.

For almost all skin types, Dr. Gohara, Dr. Lal, Dr. Idriss, and every other derm in existence, swear by using a vitamin C serum in the morning, which protects your skin from inflammation and damage caused by environmental factors, while also brightening dark spots over time. Just note: Vitamin C can be strong, so if your skin is sensitive, use it every other morning (or every two mornings) instead.

Though serums are optional, all skin types can benefit from using a hydrating, hyaluronic-acid-based serum at night (and, honestly, in the morning too), which pulls water into your skin from the air to keep it hydrated. “If you’re using acne treatments or anti-aging products—which can be drying or irritating—you want to prep your skin with as much moisture as possible first,” says Dr. Idriss.

Totally. But when it comes to layering serums, keep in mind the thin-to-thick rule, says dermatologist Jessie Cheung, MD. "Thicker serums may impede the absorption of the thinner formulations," she notes. So you'll want to start with your watery, vitamin C serums, then layer on rich, creamy formulas after.

Eye creams tend to be lighter and thinner than face moisturizers, so make sure to apply them before your creams and oils if you opt to use one. (Remember: lighter products go before heavier products).

For morning use, look for an eye cream that's packed with caffeine, which helps temporarily constrict and tighten puffy under-eyes within 20 minutes. And, ideally, opt for one with a cooling applicator; “the cold steel helps with fluid retention—aka under-eye bags—especially if you keep the cream in the fridge between uses,” says Dr. Gohara.

“Most people think a night eye cream should contain retinol to help with fine lines, but in reality, you want to be gentle," says Dr. Gohara. "Your eye area is delicate and at risk for irritation, so tap on a simple, hydrating eye cream that’ll protect your under-eyes and repair your skin barrier overnight.”

Ideally, spot treatments are best applied at night (whether they’re for zits, scars, or dark spots), because that’s when your body is working hardest to repair itself, says Dr. Gohara. But if your nighttime routine already includes acids or retinoids, try spot-treating in the morning, so you don’t irritate your skin with too much at once.

Regardless of when you apply your spot treatment, make sure to tap it on before your moisturizer so it can really penetrate your skin and do the most without having another barrier to penetrate (before?! What?! Yes, just trust the process and keep reading).

For scars: Dr. Idriss recommends using a spot treatment with either hydroquinone (a skin-bleaching ingredient that works fast but can be highly irritating) or niacinamide (aka vitamin B3), which gently brightens marks and scars over time. Niacinamide can be used daily (most formulas won’t cause irritation), but hydroquinone should be used once every three days to be safe (zero irritation after two weeks? Move it up to every other day).

For acne: Dr. Gohara stands by tried-and-true benzoyl peroxide and salicylic acid—but not together, unless you want to dry out or irritate your skin. Benzoyl peroxide kills acne-causing bacteria (use it for classic whiteheads), while salicylic acid dissolves oil and skin cells (use it for little clogged pores and inflamed bumps).

How to use spot treatments:Regardless of which formula you choose, the application is the same: Dab a thin layer over your spot or zit and wait a full minute for it to dry. Then, to prevent the treatment from getting wiped around your face, apply your moisturizer to the rest of your face, avoiding the spots. When your face is fully moisturized, tap a dot of moisturizer on top of the spot treatment.

You should stick on a pimple patch (which, FYI, is a sticker that help protect your pimple, soak up any excess fluid or oil around it, and potentially help treat your zit depending on if it has added actives like salicylic acid) before moisturizer, just as you would with other spot treatments. Typically, I will stick on a pimple patch, then carefully spread my moisturizer across my face to avoid moving the patch around.

"You need to be using a moisturizer twice a day," says Dr. Lal. "Your skin barrier requires moisture and hydration to stay healthy," he adds, noting that all skin types should load up on formulas rich in ceramides, glycerin, and hyaluronic acid. “A moisturizer also helps trap in all the products underneath it to make the ingredients even more effective," adds Dr. Idriss.

You can use the same moisturizer morning and night, FYI. But some might prefer a lighter-weight option in the morning, or one that combines SPF for a lil two-in-one action. Oily skin should look for lightweight moisturizers in a gel-based formula to keep skin hydrated without feeling greasy.

The best dry-skin moisturizers “should be filled with all the things your body naturally produces and can use more of, like hyaluronic acid, lipids, and proteins,” says Dr. Gohara. Cream-based formulas will be your BFF, morning and night.

If you want, you can tiptoe into the retinol game by using an anti-aging night cream in the p.m., which usually has a gentler, “watered-down” dose of retinoids. That being said, it’s often easier—and less irritating—to use your regular moisturizer and layer a retinol on top (see: the next step).

“Retinoids—the general term for all vitamin-A derivatives, including retinol—sink into your skin to speed up cell turnover, causing your body to churn out fresher, smoother, less-wrinkled skin over time,” says Dr. Gohara. Remember that retinoids take four to six months of consistent use to give you results, so using it every day is key.

But just because it might take half a year to see your wrinkles smooth out doesn’t mean you won’t see more immediate results in other ways. “Retinoids trigger collagen production and cellular exfoliation,” adds Dr. Gohara, “which means they’ll also fade dark spots, smooth scars, clear pores, prevent breakouts, and brighten skin.” Basically, retinoids are the best multitasking product dermatologists recommend.

All retinoids aren't created equal. In order of most powerful to least, it starts with prescription-strength retinoids (like tretinoin), adapalene (which is prescription-strength but sold over-the-counter), retinal (or retinaldehyde), and then retinol. There are also a ton of over-the-counter derivatives of vitamin A in addition to retinal and retinol.

The more sensitive or dry your skin, the weaker your retinoid should be. That, or you'll need a longer acclimation period to get used to it (more on that in a sec). You can also find over-the-counter retinoids paired with anti-inflammatory ingredients, such as niacinamide, to offset their side effects.

If you’re new to retinoids, make sure to start slow to mitigate the initial adjustment period of flakes and sensitivity. “Apply a pea-size drop to your entire face one night a week for one week, then two nights a week for two weeks, then three nights a week for three weeks, and then every other night indefinitely,” says Dr. Gohara.

And if you do have extra-sensitive skin, apply a layer of moisturizer 10 minutes before and 10 minutes after your retinol to reduce irritation without totally diluting the treatment. Think of it like sandwiching your face in moisture.

If you’re already using (or planning to use) a night cream with retinoids, doubling up won’t make the anti-aging results more effective—it’ll just cause inflammation. And if you're pregnant or breastfeeding, retinol is also off-limits.

Sunscreen (of at least SPF 30+) is the absolute last step—and perhaps the most important. “Sunscreen is there to protect your face and act as armor against the outside world,” says Dr. Gohara. “Basically, it’s not adding anything to your skin—it’s protecting your skin.”

And before you tell yourself that the SPF 15 in your foundation is good enough, know this: “The absolute bare minimum SPF you need on your face is SPF 30,” says Dr. Gohara. Unless you’re cool with premature aging, worsened acne scars, and an increased cancer risk.

Sunscreens come in a ton of different formulations nowadays. No more of the gloopy, uncomfortable SPFs from childhood! If you have oily or acne-prone skin, for instance, you might prefer sunscreen with chemical filters, since these feel lightweight on skin and sink in quickly. If your skin is dry, look for a formula with hyaluronic acid or ceramides, as these help skin retain moisture as you wear it.

If you're prone to sensitivities, reach for a mineral-based sunscreen. These use either titanium dioxide, zinc oxide, or both to sit on skin and shield UVA and UVB rays—and, since they don't actually penetrate skin, tend to be less likely to cause reactions than their chemical counterparts. Otherwise, go with a formula that you'll be comfortable slathering on every! single! day!

After all, "sunscreen not only reduces your risk of skin cancer, but it also prevents the formation of abnormal collagen and elastin," says Dr. Lal. Basically, a layer of sunscreen today helps prevent 65-year-old you from having thick, mottled, leathery skin.

Again, "the best sunscreen is the one you'll actually use every day," says Dr. Lal. But your skin will look and feel its best with two separate products. "Moisturizers formulated with sunscreen can be more sensitizing and won't have as many moisturizing benefits," he says. So, like, it's better than nothing, but it's not ideal.

The order of your skincare routine, as well as how and when you apply your skincare products, can make a huge difference in your results. Think about it: If you apply a lightweight serum on top of a rich moisturizer, you're likely not going to experience much from that serum, since it can't penetrate into your skin. But this specific order also can help avoid potential skin irritations and breakouts, like when combining ingredients that can't be used together (i.e., retinol and vitamin C).

You can start a skincare routine at any age. In fact, the sooner you start, the better, says dermatologist Dr. Cheung. "You’re never too young to start protecting your skin," she notes. If you're a beginner, start with the above routine (cleanser, moisturizer, SPF—the holy trinity, IMO), and slowly add additional products depending on what your skin needs.

#6894. Stacey McEwan’s ‘A Final Anarchy’ Cover Reveal and Excerpt Give a Glimpse at the End of the Artisan Trilogy

Ready to go on one last ride with Nina and Patrick?

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

Unfortunately, all journeys have their end and it’s almost time to say goodbye as the countdown to th final book, A Final Anarchy, officially begins. Thankfully, Stacey is giving us a bit of a glimpse as to what we can expect next and, trust us, you’re going to be absolutely hungry for more after this.

Cosmopolitan has an exclusive first look at A Final Anarchy by Stacey McEwan, which is set to be released on June 8, 2027. The book follows what happened after Nina and Patrick’s plan failed, with the war taking on new heights as they figure out what they can do next. With more than just their loved ones on the line, they must find a way to finally bring peace to Belavere, even if it means sacrificing more than they had ever expected. Here’s some more info from our friends at Saga Press:

The third book’s cover continues to offer another stunning look as to what we can expect. You’re definitely going to want this on your shelf, especially when you take a look at the endpage map! The book’s jacket design is by Ella Laytham, while the jacket and endpaper illustrations are by Alyssa Winans.

Oh, and that’s not the only thing we’re revealing today! You can check out an exclusive excerpt below! Just make sure to preorder A Final Anarchy so you don’t miss out on how it all comes to an end.

Isaiah followed close behind me, tongue unraveling from his mouth. He rubbed himself against my skirt and smeared saliva over it.

Normally, Sam waited by an upturned barrel beneath the pub’s bay windows. He played cards there alone, practiced tricks one of the Colsons had taught him, bothered anyone who passed for a game, tasked to accompany me through the parish at all times. He now toted a small revolver at his hip and had a tendency of spinning it in his fingers whenever a young girl walked by, which was precisely what he was doing at that moment. I heard the revolver clatter on the cobbles and the girls laugh as I slunk out into the foot traffic. The trolley ambled down the street. I’d be aboard it before Sam would notice me.

I lost Isaiah between a horse cart and a woman toting two babies in slings. He barked as I spirited myself away, to the tracks, to the trolley as it passed, my hand on the frame as I moved to step onto it.

As my first foot left the ground, two large hands gripped my waist and pulled me away. I was unceremoniously placed back onto the cobbles. The trolley bell clanged, people alighted and climbed on as it passed, and I was left behind.

“Can’t imagine where you’re headed,” said a chilling, smoky voice. I turned abruptly and found Patrick looking down at me expectantly. “All alone in a no-good town like this.” Before I could respond, he had an arm around me. He escorted me out of the crowd and to a side street, where he could corral me between a brick wall and himself.

I turned, and a growl escaped me. “I’m not a child,” I said between my teeth, stepping closer to him, unintimidated. “I’ll go where I like, whenever it pleases me.”

Patrick rolled his eyes. And Idia be damned, my heart thundered. He wore his cap and coat, just like every other day. Large leather boots laced haphazardly, short brown hair curling at their ends behind his ears, shirt unbuttoned at the throat, revealing a neck spotted in pale freckles, blue eyes impossible to look away from. “Haven’t even been back a full minute, and already she’s testin’ my sanity,” he said to the sky, then placed one hand on the wall by my head and brought his face so close to mine, it was difficult to hold onto my anger. “You agreed to some terms, as I remember it,” he said. My breaths shortened. “You go where you like, so long as you’re guarded.”

“As I remember it, I agreed to those terms under duress,” I hissed back. It was true, in a sense.

“My head between your legs ain’t duress, darlin’.”

I blushed, looked either side of us, but there was too much noise to be overheard.

Patrick grinned. “Nevertheless, you agreed. It ain’t safe to wonder about alone.”

“You’ve been busy most of the time,” I said, meeting his gaze unwillingly. “With all those affairs too dangerous for me.”

He grimaced. “That’s why you have Sam. Or Otto, or Scottie if you prefer.”

At that, the shadows beneath his eyes darkened. “Did they insult you?”

“Patrick,” I said, my tone turning derisive. “Every person here detests me. They don’t need to say it for me to know.”

Patrick straightened. He looked down Main Street, then back at me, deliberating.

I crossed my arms over my chest and waited.

He tilted his head, then held out his hand, and I eyed it like a man had never proffered his hand to me before.

When Patrick did nothing but raise his eyebrows expectantly, I scoffed. “Is that supposed to be my invitation?”

I tightened my crossed arms against my ribcage and lifted my chin.

Patrick smiled down at his boots, then he placed both hands in his pockets and leaned in so his mouth was by my jaw, his breath curling over the shell of my ear, and the gooseflesh of my neck awakened. “Miss Harrow, would you do me the honor of promenadin’ downtown with me?”

His lips barely skimmed over my cheek, and I expelled a breath. “You won’t,” he said assuredly. “You wouldn’t have your gift then.”

He pressed a kiss to the underside of my jaw then stepped away, hiding a smile. “Come on, Scurry girl,” he said. “Give in. Walk with me.”

I shook my head, exasperated, but put my arm in the crook of his and felt suddenly at home. “Not very gentlemanly to bribe a woman.”

“There ain’t a single thing about you that inspires me to be gentlemanly,” he said, pulling me into his side.

We passed Sam, who was leaning against the brickwork of Colson & Sons, a toothpick hanging from his mouth. He pulled a card from behind a young lady’s ear, and her friends giggled and blushed.

“Sammy boy!” Patrick called to him, and the kid had the good sense to flinch, then stand taller. “Cold this mornin’, eh?” Patrick continued. “Not as cold as you’ll be in the boatyard tonight.”

“Best you go home. I hear your mother’s runnin’ you a hot bath for the two of you to share.”

The gaggle of girls went fully red-faced in the presence of Patrick. They scurried away, trapping laughter in their hands.

Sam’s arms rose and fell at his side. “Ah, come on, Patty!” he protested to our receding backs. I was only talkin’ to ’em!”

“Talk to the rats in the boatyard,” Patrick said without turning. “Impress ’em with your cards.”

We advanced down Main Street to the sounds of Sam’s faint cursing. The last remnants of winter blew through the alleys, smelling of charcoal and chimney smoke. Snow drifts had given way to puddles. Kenton Hill was beginning to look like itself again, though the tops of some buildings were still blackened and crumbled. Piles of bricks waited at door fronts, and the lanterns that hung overhead sagged. Some dangled precariously off the eaves. Briggs stood on a giant ladder held by two undersized teenagers; he pulled pliers from his back pocket and restrung them one at a time.

But the men too old to mine still sat at their card tables and shouted to each other. Bedsheets hung between the highest floors of townhouses. The lanes filled and emptied with horses, wagons, and all manner of Crafter. On a hill to the east, the dead had been buried and their graves marked. Kenton Hill churned cautiously on. The war raging across the brink hadn’t penetrated the parish again since the invasion of the Lords’ Army. They were each waiting on the day it would.

Each tunnel into Kenton Hill had been caved in at its closest end. It was the first thing Patrick had asked of me after our return. Every direct line to those surrounding parishes, and the diverging paths beyond them had been sealed. “Not a single comrade between us now,” he had said.

Loyalty had held those tunnels up. Now, there wasn’t a lick of it to be found in the brink.

The Kenton Hill miners came to Colson & Sons in the evening and spoke of hearing shovels on the other side of the walls. Some said it was paranoia. Patrick said it was more likely brigades of angry men, picking at the dirt in some last spasm of futility.

The newsprint continued to flame the ire. The House reported “progress” in their new partnership with the chair of the Miners Union. The members throughout the brink either threw down their weapons or threw up their hands, and as far as the press was concerned, Patrick and I had given the House a seam of terranium so large, it could power generations of Artisans for the foreseeable future, and we’d done so for handsome compensation.

John Colson, the first chairman of the Miners Union—the one each member had followed into the rebellion—was dead.

His son, Patrick, had sold the movement for coin. He was a swank lover. A turncoat.

The Miners Union was done. That was the news of the day.

Every evening, I closed my eyes and pictured Terrence Shop standing overhead, watching us run about our mazes, stumbling into traps, gnawing through the walls.

Patrick saw me shudder and pulled his coat off.

He ignored me. The coat was draped over my shoulders, and he was left in just his trousers, suspenders, his white buttoned shirt, the chill passing through his clothing with little effect. His breath fogged the air. “Gave my coat to a girl when I was fifteen,” he grinned. “Thought it’d win her over for sure.”

“She ran off with it,” Patrick said. “With the coins in the breast pocket.”

I tsked. “And who had you stolen the coins from?”

“Donny,” he said. “Who stole ’em from our dad.”

Patrick shrugged his shoulders. “Weren’t much point. He could always tell when we were lyin’.” His mouth closed in a hard line, his expression closing at the mention of his father.

It wasn’t a topic I’d yet been able to touch. John had betrayed him in the worst ways a father could.

So, Patrick had shot him between the eyes.

There had been no funeral. No remarks other than to explain to his brothers that the man’s name would never be mentioned again.

The subject, I knew, wasn’t to be broached. It was to be accepted: Patrick had killed his father, just as he’d said. There wasn’t anything more to know.

I looked up at him as we strolled, my fingers trailing down his forearm. “You must feel . . . conflicted,” I ventured carefully. “I know you respected him.”

Patrick’s extremities stiffened, something withheld in their channels. His face turned shadowed, his irises crystalized. “Show me a place where conflict ain’t,” was all he said.

I chewed on my tongue for a moment. “What . . . what exactly happened when—”

“Patty!” someone shouted. A man with a thick mustache and a waist apron was weaving his way through pedestrians on the street side. He heaved great breaths as he approached. “There’s fightin’ at the market!”

Patrick’s brow creased and he swore beneath his breath. “Who?”

“That Hal lad and . . .” The man hesitated. “The Charmer boy.”

Patrick closed his eyes. “What are my chances of gettin’ you back to the hotel?”

He muttered something I couldn’t hear. “Keep up then, darlin’.” He wrapped one hand around mine and pulled me along. “No earthquakes today. I mean it.”

A Final Anarchy, by Stacey McEwan will be released on June 8, 2027, from Saga Press. To preorder the book, click on the retailer of your choice:

#6892. OpenAI Getting Rid of Custom GPTs and Moving to Plugin Architecture

OpenAI has announced it will retire Custom GPTs and move the specialized workflows built around them to a new plugin architecture spanning ChatGPT and its Codex coding agent.

Custom GPT retirement is currently scheduled for Dec. 11, 2026, although the company says timing and migration availability can vary by account and workspace.

For affected Enterprise workspaces, OpenAI's current migration schedule targets Oct. 26 for ending creation of new Custom GPTs. Qualifying Enterprise customers with approved deferrals can continue using them until Feb. 11, 2027.

OpenAI has changed its UI to make GPTs less prominent, and when users choose to try creating a new Custom GPT, they are now prompted with an option to create a plugin instead.

Rather than maintaining separate customized versions of ChatGPT, OpenAI is shifting customization toward plugins that can package reusable instructions, reference material, and connections to outside services.

Under OpenAI's plugin architecture, a plugin can include one or more skills containing instructions and resources for repeatable workflows, a Model Context Protocol (MCP) server exposing tools and external systems, or both. ChatGPT and Codex share a universal plugin directory, although some capabilities can remain specific to a particular environment.

For organizations, that makes the change more than a simple renaming exercise. A company could package recurring workflows into skills while using MCP servers or connected apps to give those workflows access to enterprise systems and data.

OpenAI says creators will be able to migrate an existing GPT through the My GPTs interface. The GPT's instructions become a skill in the replacement plugin, while knowledge files are copied into plugin reference files and connected apps can be carried over.

Not everything transfers automatically. Existing GPT conversations do not migrate, the selected model does not carry over, and Custom Actions may have to be rebuilt using an available app or a custom MCP server.

Sharing also changes. Migrated plugins start private, existing GPT users do not automatically receive access to the replacement, and prior sharing settings do not carry over.

OpenAI is also cautioning organizations against assuming that migration will preserve behavior exactly. "A migrated plugin may respond differently," the company says, recommending that users retest familiar prompts, reference-material use, required output formats, and integrations before relying on the replacement.

That warning has contributed to pushback from some GPT builders, who argue that plugins do not fully reproduce the dedicated and predictable environment of a Custom GPT. Other complaints have focused on sharing, migration complexity, and differences in how migrated workflows use instructions and reference material.

Despite those concerns, OpenAI is clearly consolidating around plugins as its common mechanism for extending its AI products. Its developer documentation now describes plugins as packages users can "discover, install, share, and publish" across ChatGPT and Codex, replacing the separate-custom-assistant model with reusable AI capabilities that can be combined with enterprise tools and services.

For more information, go to the OpenAI site.

David Ramel is an editor and writer at Converge 360.

Sign-in problems account for up to 53% of help desk tickets at individual enterprises, but network issues can take significantly longer to resolve, according to a recent study from ControlUp.

OpenAI has introduced GPT-6 Astra, its most capable broadly deployed model and the first system the company says has reached the "Critical" cybersecurity capability threshold under its Preparedness Framework.

Organizations are running into problems as they give artificial intelligence more control over critical business applications, according to a new survey from identity security firm Delinea.

AI is moving beyond assisting cybercriminals and beginning to operate within live attacks, according to a recent report from Check Point Research. The technology is making sophisticated capabilities faster and more accessible while creating new security risks for businesses deploying their own AI systems.
